The '''2020 United States House of Representatives elections''' were held on November 3, 2020, to elect representatives from all 435 [[List of United States congressional districts|congressional districts]] across each of the 50 [[U.S. state]]s to the [[117th United States Congress]], as well as six [[Delegate (United States Congress)|non-voting delegates]] from the [[District of Columbia]] and the inhabited [[Insular area|U.S. territories]]. Special House elections were also held on various dates throughout 2020.

In the [[2018 United States House of Representatives elections]], the [[Democratic Party (United States)|Democrats]] had won 235 seats. Leading up to the 2020 elections, the Democrats were projected by many polls to expand their majority by up to 15 seats due to the unpopularity of then-President [[Donald Trump]]. While Democrats ultimately retained control of the House following the 2020 elections, Republicans made a net gain of 14 seats to the surprise of most observers<ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.brookings.edu/blog/fixgov/2020/12/21/why-did-house-democrats-underperform-compared-to-joe-biden/|title=Why did House Democrats under-perform, compared to Joe Biden?|first=William A.|last=Galston|date=December 21, 2020}}</ref> and the Democrats entered 2021 with a narrow 222–213 House majority.<ref name=AP.Clinch/><ref>{{Cite web|url=https://nymag.com/intelligencer/2021/04/democrats-house-control-fragile.html|title=Democrats' Control of the House Is Increasingly Fragile|first=Ed|last=Kilgore|date=April 6, 2021|website=Intelligencer}}</ref> This was the first time since [[2004 United States House of Representatives elections|2004]] that the Republican Party made net gains in the House during a presidential election year. This led to Democrats' smallest majority since 1942. As of 2024, this remains the last time that the Democrats have won a majority in the [[United States House of Representatives]].

Republicans exceeded expectations in the 2020 House elections.  They won back a number of seats that they lost in 2018 while successfully defending competitive seats that Democrats had hoped to flip. No Republican incumbent was defeated for re-election, while 13 incumbent Democrats were ousted by Republicans; also, several successful Democratic candidates won by smaller-than-expected margins. Many have cited Trump's presence on the ballot as having fueled high Republican turnout,<ref name=AP.Clinch>{{Cite news|url=https://apnews.com/article/election-2020-politics-elections-house-elections-nancy-pelosi-1797f752392d02702429b13b1b16b553|title=Dems clinch House control, but majority likely to shrink|publisher=Associated Press|last=Fram|first=Alan|date=November 10, 2020|access-date=November 12, 2020}}</ref><ref>{{Cite news|url=https://www.cnbc.com/2020/11/05/2020-election-results-democrats-to-lose-house-seats-but-keep-majority.html|title=Democrats are projected to lose House seats but keep their majority|publisher=CNBC|last=Pramuk|first=Jacob|date=November 5, 2020|access-date=November 12, 2020}}</ref><ref>{{Cite news|url=https://www.cnn.com/2020/11/04/politics/house-race-results-2020/index.html|title=Some House Democrats fall while the party fails to flip some key suburban districts|publisher=CNN|last1=Pathe|first1=Simone|last2=Foran|first2=Clare|last3=Raju|first3=Manu|date=November 5, 2020|access-date=November 13, 2020}}</ref><ref>{{Cite news|url=https://apnews.com/article/election-2020-donald-trump-race-and-ethnicity-iowa-elections-d7c101e1432fbe49191f26d41d6c6832|title=How Democrats came up short in bid to expand House majority|publisher=Associated Press|last1=Weissert|first1=Will|last2=Beaumont|first2=Thomas|date=November 23, 2020|access-date=November 24, 2020}}</ref> while others have emphasized the Republican Party's efforts to promote their female and minority candidates.<ref>{{Cite news|last=Firozi|first=Paulina|date=November 11, 2020|title=House GOP chipped away at Democratic majority. They can thank female candidates.|newspaper=The Washington Post|url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/politics/house-republicans-women-trump/2020/11/07/a6f4ee80-205b-11eb-90dd-abd0f7086a91_story.html|access-date=2021-01-23}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web|title=How A Record Number Of Republican Women Got Elected To Congress|url=https://www.npr.org/2020/11/13/934249216/how-a-record-number-of-republican-women-got-elected-to-congress|access-date=2021-01-23|website=NPR.org|date=November 13, 2020|language=en|last1=Kurtzleben|first1=Danielle}}</ref><ref>{{Cite news|last=Swers|first=Michele L.|date=January 5, 2021|title=More Republican women than before will serve in this Congress. Here's why.|url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/politics/2021/01/05/more-republican-women-than-before-will-serve-this-congress-heres-why/|url-status=live|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210106192341/https://www.washingtonpost.com/politics/2021/01/05/more-republican-women-than-before-will-serve-this-congress-heres-why/ |archive-date=January 6, 2021 |access-date=2021-01-23|newspaper=The Washington Post}}</ref><ref>{{cite news|url=https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2020/11/18/us-republicans-won-house-seats-with-new-diversity|title=US Republicans grow more diverse in newly won House seats|publisher=Al Jazeera|last=Roberts|first=William|date=November 18, 2020|access-date=February 5, 2021}}</ref><!-- DO NOT RESTORE "Changes since previous election" SECTION UNLESS AGREED TO IN TALK PAGE -->

== Electoral system ==
Forty-seven states used the [[first-past-the-post voting]] plurality system to elect their representatives. [[Instant-runoff voting]] was used in one state (Maine) and a [[Two-round system|runoff system]] was used in two states (Georgia and Louisiana).

== Results summary ==

=== Federal ===
The 2020 election results are compared below to the November 2018 election, in which only 434 seats were filled (the election results in one constituency were voided). The results summary below does not include blank and over/under votes which were included in the official results.

== Retirements ==
[[File:US House 2020 retirements.svg|thumb|350px|Retiring incumbents by district<br>{{legend0|#92C5DE|Democratic incumbent ran}}<br/>{{legend0|#0671B0|Democratic incumbent retired or lost renomination}}<br/>{{legend0|#F48882|Republican incumbent ran}}<br/>{{legend0|#CA0120|Republican incumbent retired or lost renomination}}<br/>{{legend0|#F1C92A|Libertarian incumbent retired}}<br/>{{legend0|#6D6D6D|Vacant district}}]]

36 incumbents did not seek re-election, either to retire or to seek other positions.

=== Democrats ===
Nine Democrats did not seek re-election.
#{{ushr|CA|53|X}}: [[Susan Davis (politician)|Susan Davis]] retired.
#{{ushr|HI|2|X}}: [[Tulsi Gabbard]] retired to [[Tulsi Gabbard 2020 presidential campaign|run for U.S. president]].
#{{ushr|IN|1|X}}: [[Pete Visclosky]] retired.
#{{ushr|IA|2|X}}: [[Dave Loebsack]] retired.
#{{ushr|MA|4|X}}: [[Joe Kennedy III]] retired to [[2020 United States Senate election in Massachusetts|run for the U.S. Senate]]. 
#{{ushr|NM|3|X}}: [[Ben Ray Luján]] retired to [[2020 United States Senate election in New Mexico|run for the U.S. Senate]].
#{{ushr|NY|15|X}}: [[José E. Serrano|José Serrano]] retired.
#{{ushr|NY|17|X}}: [[Nita Lowey]] retired.
#{{ushr|WA|10|X}}: [[Denny Heck]] retired to [[2020 Washington lieutenant gubernatorial election|run for lieutenant governor of Washington]].

=== Libertarians ===
One Libertarian did not seek re-election.
#{{ushr|MI|3|X}}: [[Justin Amash]] retired.

== Resignations ==
Four incumbents resigned in 2020, all of them Republicans, with no special elections to fill the vacant seats before the November election.
<!-- THIS SECTION IS FOR OCCURRENCES IN THE YEAR 2020 ONLY! -->

=== Republicans ===
# {{ushr|CA|50|X}}: [[Duncan D. Hunter|Duncan Hunter]] resigned January 13 after pleading guilty to one count of misusing campaign funds. Seat won by Republican [[Darrell Issa]].<ref>{{Cite web |last=Dzhanova |first=Yelena |url=https://www.cnbc.com/2020/01/07/duncan-hunter-resigns-after-pleading-guilty-to-misusing-campaign-funds.html |title=Rep. Duncan Hunter resigns from the House, weeks after pleading guilty to misusing campaign funds |publisher=CNBC |date=January 7, 2020}}</ref>
# {{ushr|GA|14|X}}: [[Tom Graves]] resigned October 4; he had initially planned to retire at the end of the term. Seat won by Republican [[Marjorie Taylor Greene]].<ref>{{Cite news|title=Georgia Rep. Tom Graves to resign, effective October|last=McPherson|first=Lindsey|date=September 11, 2020|work=Roll Call|location=Washington, D.C.|url=https://www.rollcall.com/2020/09/11/georgia-rep-tom-graves-to-resign-effective-october/|access-date=September 16, 2020}}</ref>
# {{ushr|NC|11|X}}: [[Mark Meadows (North Carolina politician)|Mark Meadows]] resigned March 30 to become [[White House Chief of Staff]]. Seat won by Republican [[Madison Cawthorn]].<ref>{{Cite web |last=Lesniewski |first=Niels |url=https://www.rollcall.com/2020/03/30/rep-mark-meadows-resigns-to-become-white-house-chief-of-staff/ |title=Rep. Mark Meadows resigns to become White House chief of staff |publisher=Roll Call |date=March 30, 2020}}</ref>
# {{ushr|TX|4|X}}: [[John Ratcliffe (American politician)|John Ratcliffe]] resigned May 22 to become [[Director of National Intelligence]]. Seat won by Republican [[Pat Fallon]].<ref>{{Cite web |last=Gillman |first=Todd J. |url=https://www.dallasnews.com/news/politics/2020/05/21/senate-poised-to-confirm-rep-john-ratcliffe-as-director-of-national-intelligence/ |title=Divided Senate confirms Texas Rep. John Ratcliffe as director of national intelligence |publisher=The Dallas Morning News |date=May 21, 2020}}</ref>

== Incumbents defeated ==

=== In primary elections ===
Eight incumbents lost renomination in 2020, the most in a non-redistricting year since 1974.<ref>{{Cite news|url=https://www.msnbc.com/rachel-maddow-show/yet-another-house-republican-incumbent-loses-primary-n1237265|title=Yet another House Republican incumbent loses in a primary|publisher=MSNBC|last=Benen|first=Steve|author-link=Steve Benen|date=August 19, 2020|access-date=August 30, 2020}}</ref>

==== Democrats ====
Three Democrats lost renomination.
# {{ushr|IL|3|X}}: [[Dan Lipinski]] lost renomination to [[Marie Newman]], who went on to win the general election.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Mutnick |first=Ally |url=https://www.politico.com/news/2020/03/18/rep-dan-lipinski-falls-in-democratic-primary-135175 |title=Rep. Dan Lipinski falls in Democratic primary |publisher=Politico |date=March 18, 2020}}</ref>
# {{ushr|MO|1|X}}: [[Lacy Clay]] lost renomination to [[Cori Bush]], who went on to win the general election.<ref>{{Cite web|last=Fandos|first=Nicholas|url=https://www.nytimes.com/2020/08/05/us/politics/cori-bush-missouri-william-lacy-clay.html|title=Cori Bush Defeats William Lacy Clay in a Show of Progressive Might|work=The New York Times|date=August 5, 2020}}</ref>
# {{ushr|NY|16|X}}: [[Eliot Engel]] lost renomination to [[Jamaal Bowman]], who went on to win the general election.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Hayes |first=Christal |url=https://www.usatoday.com/story/news/politics/elections/2020/07/17/jamaal-bowman-ousts-powerful-house-chair-rep-eliot-engel-new-york/5348924002/ |title=Powerful House chair Eliot Engel defeated by progressive newcomer Jamaal Bowman in stunning upset |publisher=USAToday |date=July 17, 2020}}</ref>

==== Republicans ====
Five Republicans lost renomination.
# {{ushr|CO|3|X}}: [[Scott Tipton]] lost renomination to [[Lauren Boebert]], who won the general election.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Harsha |first=Keagan |url=https://kdvr.com/news/politics/colorado-primary-winner-lauren-boebert-meets-president-trump-distances-herself-from-qanon/ |title=Colorado primary winner Lauren Boebert meets President Trump, distances herself from QAnon |publisher=KDVR |date=July 6, 2020 |access-date=July 26, 2020 |archive-date=August 4, 2020 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20200804173234/https://kdvr.com/news/politics/colorado-primary-winner-lauren-boebert-meets-president-trump-distances-herself-from-qanon/ |url-status=dead }}</ref>
# {{ushr|FL|15|X}}: [[Ross Spano]] lost renomination to [[Scott Franklin (politician)|Scott Franklin]], who won the general election.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Irwin Taylor |first=Janelle |url=https://floridapolitics.com/archives/358834-scott-franklin-ousts-ross-spano-in-cd-15-will-take-on-alan-cohn-in-november |title=Scott Franklin ousts Ross Spano in CD 15, will take on Alan Cohn in November |publisher=Florida Politics |date=August 18, 2020}}</ref>
# {{ushr|IA|4|X}}: [[Steve King]] lost renomination to [[Randy Feenstra]], who won the general election.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Sprunt |first=Barbara |url=https://www.npr.org/2020/06/03/865823546/iowa-rep-steve-king-ousted-in-gop-primary-ap-projects |title=Iowa Rep. Steve King, Known For Racist Comments, Loses Reelection Bid |publisher=NPR |date=June 3, 2020}}</ref>
# {{ushr|KS|2|X}}: [[Steve Watkins]] lost renomination to [[Jake LaTurner]], who won the general election.<ref>{{Cite web |last1=Panetta |first1=Grace |title=Embattled Kansas congressman Steve Watkins is ousted by primary challenger Jake LaTurner |url=https://www.businessinsider.com/steve-watkins-vs-jake-laturner-kansas-primary-live-results-2020-7 |website=Business Insider}}</ref>
# {{ushr|VA|5|X}}: [[Denver Riggleman]] lost renomination in a district convention to [[Bob Good]], who won the general election.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Mutnick |first=Ally |url=https://www.politico.com/news/2020/06/14/rep-denver-riggleman-ousted-in-virginia-gop-convention-316589 |title=Rep. Denver Riggleman ousted in Virginia GOP convention |publisher=Politico |date=June 14, 2020}}</ref>

=== In general elections ===
==== Democrats ====
Thirteen Democrats, twelve of whom were freshmen, lost re-election to Republicans.
# {{ushr|CA|21|X}}: [[TJ Cox]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[David Valadao]].
# {{ushr|CA|39|X}}: [[Gil Cisneros]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Young Kim]].
# {{ushr|CA|48|X}}: [[Harley Rouda]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Michelle Steel]].
# {{ushr|FL|26|X}}: [[Debbie Mucarsel-Powell]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Carlos A. Giménez|Carlos Giménez]].
# {{ushr|FL|27|X}}: [[Donna Shalala]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Maria Elvira Salazar]].
# {{ushr|IA|1|X}}: [[Abby Finkenauer]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Ashley Hinson]].
# {{ushr|MN|7|X}}: [[Collin Peterson]] (first elected in 1990) lost to [[Michelle Fischbach]].
# {{ushr|NM|2|X}}: [[Xochitl Torres Small]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Yvette Herrell]].
# {{ushr|NY|11|X}}: [[Max Rose]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Nicole Malliotakis]].
# {{ushr|NY|22|X}}: [[Anthony Brindisi]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Claudia Tenney]].
# {{ushr|OK|5|X}}: [[Kendra Horn]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Stephanie Bice]].
# {{ushr|SC|1|X}}: [[Joe Cunningham (American politician)|Joe Cunningham]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Nancy Mace]].
# {{ushr|UT|4|X}}: [[Ben McAdams]] (first elected in 2018) lost to [[Burgess Owens]].

==== Republicans ====
No Republicans lost re-election.
